Traps to Avoid (The "Disqualification" Risks) 
●​ The "Hardcoded If-Else" Trap: The system must generate the interface and data 
handling directly from field and workflow configuration with "no per-site hand-coding". 
Writing conditional code (e.g., if (site === 'service-centre') loadRepair()) fails the core 
challenge of making one codebase behave like several products purely through 
configuration. 
●​ The Scope Creep Trap: The scope is strictly frozen to the 13 approved modules, 
and adding new modules requires guide approval. Teams are explicitly instructed to 
"invest in depth rather than breadth," meaning unauthorized extra features will waste 
time and likely incur penalties. 
●​ The "Color Palette" Trap: Switching sites must change the layout, theme, modules, 
and workflows, "not just the colours". Swapping CSS variables for a light/dark mode 
is insufficient; the DOM structure and UI layout must physically adapt to the JSONB 
configuration. 
●​ The Server Restart Trap: Adding a module to a live site and publishing it must work 
with "no code changes and no restart". If applying the new React Flow configuration 
requires restarting the Node.js backend or rebuilding the React frontend, it fails this 
requirement. 
●​ The "Developer-Only Builder" Trap: The configuration tool must be usable by a 
non-developer. Exposing raw JSON editors or forcing users to understand database 
schemas violates the rule that "a builder that only developers can use misses the 
point". 
●​ The Untyped Canvas Trap: Connections within the visual builder must be strictly 
typed. Allowing arbitrary connections on the canvas—such as adding an "Approval" 
module without a preceding "Request" module—will fail the builder objectives. 
Important Points to Prioritize (The Winning Criteria) 
●​ Strict Data Isolation: Each site's data, roles, and APIs must be isolated using 
site_id, which must be enforced at both the ORM and database levels in 
PostgreSQL. Every endpoint must have a test proving that this isolation holds and 
data never leaks across sites. 
●​ Transactional Integrity: Stock updates must be strictly transactional so that an 
approval or assignment "can never leave inventory in an inconsistent state". True 
database transactions (ACID BEGIN/COMMIT blocks with row locking) are required. 
●​ The Single Renderer Engine: The builder must save each site's configuration as 
JSONB, and a single universal renderer must map that configuration into the final 
user interface. 
●​ Dynamic Calculated Fields: The data flow must support calculated fields (e.g., 
TOTAL = QTY x PRICE) combined with schema validation. 
●​ The Rigid Demo Script: Do not improvise the final presentation; the live demo must 
strictly follow the provided 4-step script. This includes logging in once, comparing two 
distinct sites (Service Centre vs. Central Stores), live-publishing the "Returns" 
module with no code changes, and demonstrating role-restricted Excel data handling. 
●​ Offline Backup Preparedness: The expected deliverables include seeded demo 
data and an offline backup specifically designed so the demo works even if the live 
setup fails.

----- PAGE 3 -----
1. The "Panic Button" (Offline Fallback) This feature transforms the mandatory offline 
backup requirement from a simple database file into a robust, product-centric offline caching 
system. 
●​ How it is Architected: 
○​ State Persistence: Integrate the zustand/middleware/persist module and 
map its storage engine to IndexedDB (using a lightweight wrapper like 
idb-keyval). This circumvents the 5MB limit of standard localStorage. 
○​ Network Interceptor: Create an Axios interceptor that listens to 
window.navigator.onLine. If an API read request fails due to network loss, the 
interceptor catches the error and silently directs the UI to read from the 
Zustand cache instead of crashing. 
○​ Visual Indicators: Wrap your Dashboard Shell in a network context provider. 
When the network drops, a global Tailwind banner dynamically renders at the 
top: "Offline Mode - Viewing Cached Data." 
●​ What it does during the Demo: 
○​ During the final presentation, physically turn off your laptop's Wi-Fi. 
○​ Show the judges the app detecting the drop instantly and displaying the 
offline banner. 
○​ Navigate between the Inventory, PCB Repair, and Analytics Dashboard 
modules to prove the UI still renders perfectly using the locally cached 
IndexedDB data, ensuring the demo survives any live setup failure. 
2. Dual-Layer PostgreSQL RLS + Live "Pen-Test" Proof This feature provides visual, 
undeniable proof that you have achieved the core objective of data isolation enforced at both 
the ORM and database levels. 
●​ How it is Architected: 
○​ Database Level (PostgreSQL): Enable Row-Level Security on all 
multi-tenant tables. 
○​ SQL 
ALTER TABLE inventory ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY; 
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON inventory  
USING (site_id = current_setting('app.current_site_id')::uuid); 
○​ 
○​ 
○​ ORM Level (Node/Express): Create a middleware function that extracts the 
site_id from the JWT. Before executing any business query, it runs await 
db.query("SET LOCAL app.current_site_id = $1", [req.user.siteId]); within a 
transaction. 
○​ The Sandbox UI: Build a specific "Isolation Test" modal in the Hub layer. 
●​ What it does during the Demo: 
○​ Open the "Isolation Test" modal. 
○​ Click "Run Attack." The React frontend will visually execute a forged Axios 
request where a user authenticated for /central-stores attempts to query 
/api/service-centre/requests. 


----- PAGE 4 -----
○​ Display the raw server response showing an empty array or 403 Forbidden 
generated by the database engine itself, proving that shared modules never 
leak data. 
3. Cross-Site Topology-Aware Search This directly solves the business problem where 
admins have no combined view across units by aggregating isolated data intelligently. 
●​ How it is Architected: 
○​ Hub Search API: Create a dedicated Node/Express endpoint /api/hub/search 
that is strictly protected by Admin role-based access. 
○​ Aggregated Querying: The backend utilizes PostgreSQL UNION ALL or 
tsvector full-text search across the Inventory, Requests, and PCB Repair 
tables, filtering only for the specific site_ids the Admin manages. 
○​ Data Tagging: The API formats the response to include the source topology 
(e.g., item: 'Resistor', source: '/central-stores', module: 'Inventory'). 
●​ What it does during the Demo: 
○​ From the central Hub layer, type a keyword into the global search bar. 
○​ The dropdown categorizes the results using visually distinct Tailwind badges 
representing their origin module. 
○​ Clicking a search result utilizes React Router to bypass the standard 
navigation, deep-linking the Admin directly into the correct site and specific 
module row. 
4. "Aging SLA" (Service Level Agreement) Indicators This feature converts a static 
workflow into an active operations monitor, elevating the Request, Approval, and Assignment 
chain. 
●​ How it is Architected: 
○​ Timestamping: Ensure the requests and approvals database tables contain 
created_at and updated_at columns. 
○​ Dynamic Thresholding: In the Zustand store for the Approval module, define 
an SLA limit (e.g., 24 hours for production, but set to 10 seconds for demo 
purposes). 
○​ Conditional Rendering: In the React component rendering the Approval 
queue, map over the rows. If Date.now() - row.created_at > 
SLA_THRESHOLD, apply a bg-red-100 border-red-500 Tailwind styling. 
●​ What it does during the Demo: 
○​ Generate a new material request in the UI. 
○​ Switch to the Manager's Approval view. 
○​ Wait 10 seconds. The row will dynamically flash and turn red, appending an 
"⚠️ SLA Breached" badge to the record without requiring a page refresh. This 
visually proves the workflow engine tracks state over time.

federation / micro-frontends are not required. If the PS requires them, tell 
me, because it changes the schedule.

Problem 
Fix 
Different tables have different 
columns, so UNION ALL fails. 
Select the same columns from each table: module, 
id, site_id, title, subtitle. 
Running it as a service role 
bypasses RLS and leaks sites the 
admin does not manage.

sites.

needed.

2.4 Aging SLA 
Problem 
Fix 
Date.now() - created_at uses 
the client clock. A wrong laptop clock 
breaks the demo. 
Compute age on the server (now() - 
status_changed_at) and send age_seconds. 
The row will not turn red by itself 
after 10 seconds. React only 
re-renders on state change. 
Add a useNow(1000) hook with setInterval, 
so the component updates every second. 
created_at is the wrong start time 
after a re-assignment. 
Add status_changed_at. The SLA clock runs 
from the last status change. 
SLA limit hardcoded in a store. 
Read SLA_SECONDS from server config 
(/api/config). Production 86400, demo 10. 
Say this out loud in the demo. 
Approved rows should not breach. 
Apply SLA only to status = 'PENDING'.

3. Architecture 
React (Vite) ── Axios ──► Express API ──► PostgreSQL (RLS on) 
  │  Zustand+IndexedDB        │  JWT auth → role + site_ids 
  │  Service worker           │  withTenant(): BEGIN; set_config; query; COMMIT 
  └ Hub layer (admin)         └ /api/health, /api/config, /api/hub/search, /api/demo/* 
 
Folder layout: 
opshub/ 
  server/ 
    src/ 
      db/{pool.js, withTenant.js} 
      middleware/{auth.js, requireRole.js, requireSite.js} 
      routes/{auth,inventory,requests,approvals,pcb,analytics,hub,demo,health}.js 
    migrations/{001_schema.sql, 002_rls.sql, 003_seed.sql} 
    scripts/{backup.sh, restore.sh} 
    tests/rls.test.js 
  web/ 
    src/ 
      api/client.js            (Axios + interceptors) 
      store/{persist.js, session.js, requests.js, inventory.js} 
      hooks/{useNow.js, useOnline.js} 
      components/{OfflineBanner, SlaBadge, SearchBar, IsolationTestModal} 
      pages/{Hub, CentralStores, ServiceCentre, Analytics} 
  docker-compose.yml 
 
 
4. Build blocks (copy-ready) 
4.1 Database: roles, schema, RLS (002_rls.sql) 
-- run as the owner role (not the app role) 
CREATE ROLE app_user LOGIN PASSWORD 'change-me' NOSUPERUSER 
NOBYPASSRLS; 
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO app_user; 
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO 
app_user; 
GRANT USAGE, SELECT ON ALL SEQUENCES IN SCHEMA public TO app_user; 
 
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION app_site_ids() RETURNS uuid[] 
LANGUAGE sql STABLE AS $$ 
  SELECT COALESCE( 
    string_to_array(NULLIF(current_setting('app.site_ids', true), ''), ',')::uuid[], 
    '{}'::uuid[]) 
$$; 


----- PAGE 10 -----
 
-- repeat for: inventory, requests, approvals, assignments, pcb_repairs 
ALTER TABLE inventory ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY; 
ALTER TABLE inventory FORCE  ROW LEVEL SECURITY; 
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON inventory 
  USING      (site_id = ANY (app_site_ids())) 
  WITH CHECK (site_id = ANY (app_site_ids())); 
 
Tables (minimum): sites(id, slug, name), users(id, email, password_hash, 
role), user_sites(user_id, site_id), inventory(id, site_id, sku, name, 
qty), requests(id, site_id, item, qty, status, created_at, 
status_changed_at), approvals, assignments, pcb_repairs(id, site_id, 
...). Every tenant table has site_id uuid NOT NULL. 
4.2 Server: tenant wrapper (withTenant.js) 
export async function withTenant(user, fn) { 
  const client = await pool.connect(); 
  try { 
    await client.query('BEGIN'); 
    await client.query("SELECT set_config('app.site_ids', $1, true)", [user.siteIds.join(',')]); 
    const out = await fn(client); 
    await client.query('COMMIT'); 
    return out; 
  } catch (e) { await client.query('ROLLBACK'); throw e; } 
  finally { client.release(); } 
} 
 
Rules: 
●​ siteIds come from the signed JWT, never from the request body. 
●​ Every route uses withTenant. No route uses pool.query directly. 
●​ requireSite middleware returns 403 when the URL site is not in user.siteIds 
(this is the app layer). 
4.3 Isolation test (demo mode only, behind DEMO_MODE=true) 
●​ Test A, app layer: the modal sends a forged request, user logged in for Central 
Stores calling /api/service-centre/requests. Show the raw response: 403 
from requireSite. 
●​ Test B, database layer: GET /api/demo/attack/db runs SELECT count(*) 
FROM requests with no WHERE clause, inside withTenant, and also returns the 
real total from an owner connection. Show: rows_in_table: 120, 


----- PAGE 11 -----
rows_visible_to_you: 34. This proves the database filters even when app code 
forgets. 
●​ Show Test C in a terminal: psql as app_user without the setting returns 0 rows 
(fails closed). 
4.4 Offline cache (store/persist.js) 
import { get, set, del } from 'idb-keyval'; 
export const idbStorage = { 
  getItem: async (k) => (await get(k)) ?? null, 
  setItem: (k, v) => set(k, v), 
  removeItem: (k) => del(k), 
}; 
// store: persist(config, { name: `opshub-${userId}`, storage: createJSONStorage(() => 
idbStorage) }) 
 
Connectivity (hooks/useOnline.js): fetch('/api/health') every 5 s, plus 
online/offline events. Interceptor: if !error.response, set offline = true. On 
logout, delete the user's key. 
Service worker: vite-plugin-pwa with registerType: 'autoUpdate' and the app 
shell precached. 
4.5 SLA 
Server query: 
SELECT r.*, 
       EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM (now() - r.status_changed_at)) AS age_seconds 
FROM requests r WHERE r.status = 'PENDING'; 
 
Client: useNow(1000) re-renders every second. remaining = slaSeconds - 
(row.age_seconds + (now - fetchedAt)/1000). When remaining < 0, apply 
bg-red-100 border-red-500 and show a "SLA breached" badge (text plus colour, not 
colour alone). Refetch every 10 s to resync with the server. 
4.6 Cross-site search 
SELECT 'inventory' AS module, i.id, i.site_id, i.name AS title, i.sku AS subtitle 
  FROM inventory i WHERE i.name ILIKE $1 OR i.sku ILIKE $1 
UNION ALL 
SELECT 'request', r.id, r.site_id, r.item, r.status FROM requests r WHERE r.item ILIKE $1 
UNION ALL 
SELECT 'pcb_repair', p.id, p.site_id, p.title, p.status FROM pcb_repairs p WHERE p.title 
ILIKE $1 
LIMIT 50;

{module}?highlight=${id}).

●​ scripts/restore.sh: pg_restore --clean --if-exists -d 
"$TARGET_URL" file.dump 
●​ Admin button "Download backup" streams the latest dump (Admin only). 
●​ Test the restore on a fresh database before the demo.

3.​ Analytics beyond 3 charts

7. Demo script (5 minutes) 
1.​ Login as Central Stores user; create a material request. 
2.​ Switch to Manager approvals; wait 10 s; the row turns red with "SLA breached". 
3.​ Open Isolation Test; run Test A (403) and Test B (34 of 120 rows). Show Test C in 
psql. 
4.​ Login as Hub Admin; search a keyword; click a result; land on the highlighted row. 
5.​ Stop the backend; the banner appears; navigate Inventory → PCB Repair → 
Analytics from the cache. 
6.​ Restart the backend; the banner disappears. Show the backup file and the restore 
command. 
Prepare a recorded fallback video of steps 1–6 in case the live demo fails. 
 
8. Pre-demo checklist 
●​ [ ] App connects as app_user, not postgres (check SELECT current_user, 
rolbypassrls FROM pg_roles WHERE rolname = current_user;) 
●​ [ ] tests/rls.test.js passes: user A cannot read B; no setting returns 0 rows; 
insert into another site is rejected by WITH CHECK 
●​ [ ] SLA threshold set to 10 s via SLA_SECONDS for the demo, 86400 documented as 
default 
●​ [ ] Restore tested on a clean database 
●​ [ ] Offline demo tested by stopping the server, and by reload while offline 
●​ [ ] Cache cleared on logout (log in as a second user and check)

2. What is wrong or risky in each feature, and the fix 
2.1 Panic Button (offline) 
Problem 
Why it matters 
Fix 
A browser cache is 
not a "database 
backup". 
If the PS asks for an 
offline backup file, the 
cache does not satisfy 
it. 
Do both: (a) client cache for UX, (b) a 
real server-side backup (pg_dump) 
with a tested restore script and an 
admin "Download backup" button. 
navigator.onLine 
is unreliable. 
It reads true on a LAN 
with no internet. 
Treat a request that fails with no 
response as offline. Add a 
/api/health ping every 5 s. Use the 
browser online/offline events only 
as a hint. 
Turning off Wi-Fi 
may not break your 
demo. 
If the backend runs on 
localhost, requests 
still work and the banner 
never appears. 
Demo by stopping the backend 
process (or use DevTools → Network 
→ Offline). Stopping the server is a 
stronger story: "the server died, the 
hub still works". 
A page reload while 
offline gives a blank 
page. 
Zustand only stores 
data, not the app code. 
Add a service worker 
(vite-plugin-pwa) to precache the 
app shell. 
Cached data leaks 
between users. 
The cache outlives 
logout. This contradicts 
your isolation claim. 
Key the cache by user id and clear it 
on logout. 
Offline writes. 
They need a queue, 
conflict handling, 
idempotency. 
MVP is read-only offline. Disable 
write buttons with a tooltip "Offline". 
Queue writes only as a stretch. 
Correct design: 
●​ zustand/middleware persist with createJSONStorage(() => 
idbStorage), where idbStorage wraps idb-keyval (get, set, del). 
●​ Axios response interceptor: on !error.response, set offline = true in the 
store and return the cached data. Do not reject. 
●​ Banner reads offline from the store.

Problem 
Why it 
matters 
Fix

not accept 
bind 
parameters
.

RLS is skipped for the table owner

If the app 
connects

postgres, 
RLS does

your demo 
proves

Run migrations as an owner role.

with NOSUPERUSER

LEVEL SECURITY.

A missing

becomes a

e_ids', true). It returns 
NULL, so the policy hides every 
row (fails closed). 
One site_id per user. 
The Hub

SET without LOCAL/transaction on a 
pooled connection. 
Tenant id 
leaks to the 
next 
request 
using that

2.3 Cross-site search 
Problem 
Fix 
Different tables have different 
columns, so UNION ALL fails. 
Select the same columns from each table: module, 
id, site_id, title, subtitle. 
Running it as a service role 
bypasses RLS and leaks sites the 
admin does not manage. 
Run inside the same withTenant transaction as 
every other query. RLS then filters by the admin's 
sites. 
tsvector is more setup than 
needed. 
Use ILIKE with a debounce. Add pg_trgm only if 
you have time. 
"Bypass standard navigation" is not 
a real feature. 
It is just 
navigate('/s/:slug/:module?highlight=:i
d'). The page scrolls to the row and pulses it. 
2.4 Aging SLA 
Problem 
Fix 
Date.now() - created_at uses 
the client clock. A wrong laptop clock 
breaks the demo. 
Compute age on the server (now() - 
status_changed_at) and send age_seconds. 
The row will not turn red by itself 
after 10 seconds. React only 
re-renders on state change. 
Add a useNow(1000) hook with setInterval, 
so the component updates every second. 
created_at is the wrong start time 
after a re-assignment. 
Add status_changed_at. The SLA clock runs 
from the last status change. 
SLA limit hardcoded in a store. 
Read SLA_SECONDS from server config 
(/api/config). Production 86400, demo 10. 
Say this out loud in the demo. 
Approved rows should not breach. 
Apply SLA only to status = 'PENDING'.

3. Architecture 
React (Vite) ── Axios ──► Express API ──► PostgreSQL (RLS on) 
  │  Zustand+IndexedDB        │  JWT auth → role + site_ids 
  │  Service worker           │  withTenant(): BEGIN; set_config; query; COMMIT 
  └ Hub layer (admin)         └ /api/health, /api/config, /api/hub/search, /api/demo/* 
 
Folder layout:

  server/ 
    src/

      middleware/{auth.js, requireRole.js, requireSite.js} 
      routes/{auth,inventory,requests,approvals,pcb,analytics,hub,demo,health}.js

    tests/rls.test.js 
  web/ 
    src/

4. Build blocks (copy-ready) 
4.1 Database: roles, schema, RLS (002_rls.sql) 
-- run as the owner role (not the app role) 
CREATE ROLE app_user LOGIN PASSWORD 'change-me' NOSUPERUSER 
NOBYPASSRLS; 
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO app_user; 
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO 
app_user; 
GRANT USAGE, SELECT ON ALL SEQUENCES IN SCHEMA public TO app_user; 
 
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION app_site_ids() RETURNS uuid[] 
LANGUAGE sql STABLE AS $$ 
  SELECT COALESCE( 
    string_to_array(NULLIF(current_setting('app.site_ids', true), ''), ',')::uuid[], 
    '{}'::uuid[]) 
$$; 


----- PAGE 21 -----
 
-- repeat for: inventory, requests, approvals, assignments, pcb_repairs 
ALTER TABLE inventory ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY; 
ALTER TABLE inventory FORCE  ROW LEVEL SECURITY; 
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON inventory 
  USING      (site_id = ANY (app_site_ids())) 
  WITH CHECK (site_id = ANY (app_site_ids())); 
 
Tables (minimum): sites(id, slug, name), users(id, email, password_hash, 
role), user_sites(user_id, site_id), inventory(id, site_id, sku, name, 
qty), requests(id, site_id, item, qty, status, created_at, 
status_changed_at), approvals, assignments, pcb_repairs(id, site_id, 
...). Every tenant table has site_id uuid NOT NULL. 
4.2 Server: tenant wrapper (withTenant.js) 
export async function withTenant(user, fn) { 
  const client = await pool.connect(); 
  try { 
    await client.query('BEGIN'); 
    await client.query("SELECT set_config('app.site_ids', $1, true)", [user.siteIds.join(',')]); 
    const out = await fn(client); 
    await client.query('COMMIT'); 
    return out; 
  } catch (e) { await client.query('ROLLBACK'); throw e; } 
  finally { client.release(); } 
} 
 
Rules: 
●​ siteIds come from the signed JWT, never from the request body. 
●​ Every route uses withTenant. No route uses pool.query directly. 
●​ requireSite middleware returns 403 when the URL site is not in user.siteIds 
(this is the app layer). 
4.3 Isolation test (demo mode only, behind DEMO_MODE=true) 
●​ Test A, app layer: the modal sends a forged request, user logged in for Central 
Stores calling /api/service-centre/requests. Show the raw response: 403 
from requireSite. 
●​ Test B, database layer: GET /api/demo/attack/db runs SELECT count(*) 
FROM requests with no WHERE clause, inside withTenant, and also returns the 
real total from an owner connection. Show: rows_in_table: 120, 


----- PAGE 22 -----
rows_visible_to_you: 34. This proves the database filters even when app code 
forgets. 
●​ Show Test C in a terminal: psql as app_user without the setting returns 0 rows 
(fails closed). 
4.4 Offline cache (store/persist.js) 
import { get, set, del } from 'idb-keyval'; 
export const idbStorage = { 
  getItem: async (k) => (await get(k)) ?? null, 
  setItem: (k, v) => set(k, v), 
  removeItem: (k) => del(k), 
}; 
// store: persist(config, { name: `opshub-${userId}`, storage: createJSONStorage(() => 
idbStorage) }) 
 
Connectivity (hooks/useOnline.js): fetch('/api/health') every 5 s, plus 
online/offline events. Interceptor: if !error.response, set offline = true. On 
logout, delete the user's key. 
Service worker: vite-plugin-pwa with registerType: 'autoUpdate' and the app 
shell precached. 
4.5 SLA 
Server query: 
SELECT r.*, 
       EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM (now() - r.status_changed_at)) AS age_seconds 
FROM requests r WHERE r.status = 'PENDING'; 
 
Client: useNow(1000) re-renders every second. remaining = slaSeconds - 
(row.age_seconds + (now - fetchedAt)/1000). When remaining < 0, apply 
bg-red-100 border-red-500 and show a "SLA breached" badge (text plus colour, not 
colour alone). Refetch every 10 s to resync with the server. 
4.6 Cross-site search 
SELECT 'inventory' AS module, i.id, i.site_id, i.name AS title, i.sku AS subtitle 
  FROM inventory i WHERE i.name ILIKE $1 OR i.sku ILIKE $1 
UNION ALL 
SELECT 'request', r.id, r.site_id, r.item, r.status FROM requests r WHERE r.item ILIKE $1 
UNION ALL 
SELECT 'pcb_repair', p.id, p.site_id, p.title, p.status FROM pcb_repairs p WHERE p.title 
ILIKE $1 
LIMIT 50;

{module}?highlight=${id}).

----- PAGE 31 -----
 
 
●​ What exactly does “offline backup” require? 
●​ Do modules need to be separate deployable units? 
●​ How many sites and roles must the demo show? 
●​ Is a deployed URL required? 
 
 
Offline backup requirement: The offline backup requires seeded demo data and an offline 
fallback specifically designed so the live demo continues working even if the live setup fails. 
Module deployment: No, modules do not need to be separate deployable units. The entire 
system must run from "one codebase". Modules act as "reusable typed nodes in a module 
registry". The builder saves this configuration as JSONB, and a single renderer produces 
every site's interface. 
Demo sites and roles: The live demo script requires showing "all three units" (Service 
Centre, Central Stores, and Distribution). You must support and demonstrate four specific 
roles: Public, Member, Manager, and Admin. 
Deployed URL requirement: The provided problem statement does not explicitly require a 
public deployed URL. It mandates a "live demo" of the "live setup" and explicitly requires the 
offline backup so the presentation can succeed if the live setup fails. 
 


----- PAGE 32 -----
OpsHub: Complete Solution Document 
Genesis 2.0 · PS-03 · One codebase. Many different sites. Zero per-site code. 
 
1. The problem and what the organizer is judging 
1.1 The problem 
A company runs three units: Central Stores (inventory), Service Centre (requests, approvals, 
repairs, returns) and Distribution (material requests, assignments, consumption). All three 
need the same basic features, but each wants different fields, rules and layouts. So each unit 
gets its own codebase. Three units rebuilding 12 modules means 36 implementations where 
12 shared ones would do. 
The consequences are separate logins for users, no combined view for admins, process 
owners who can’t change a form or approval chain without a developer, shared code edits that 
break other sites, and releases with no preview or rollback. 
1.2 What the organizer expects 
Build one codebase that runs N completely different sites with three layers: a Hub (login, 
site switcher, users, themes, module catalogue), a Site layer (each site is {siteId, slug, theme, 
modules, pages, roles}), and a Module layer (13 typed nodes with defined inputs and 
outputs). 
1.3 The traps that disqualify 
Trap 
Meaning 
Hardcoded if/else 
Any if (site === 'service-centre') fails the core idea 
Scope creep 
The 13 modules are frozen. Depth beats breadth 
Colour-palette trap 
Switching sites must change layout, modules and workflow, not 
just colours 
Server restart trap 
Publishing a module must need no code change and no restart 
Developer-only 
builder 
A raw JSON editor misses the point 
Untyped canvas 
Approval must not connect without Request 
1.4 The winning criteria 


----- PAGE 33 -----
Strict site_id isolation at ORM and database level with a test per endpoint, true ACID 
transactions with row locking for stock, one universal renderer driven by JSONB, calculated 
fields with schema validation, a rigid four-step demo, and seeded data plus an offline backup. 
 
2. Our solution in one page 
OpsHub is a configuration brain with a universal body. 
An admin designs a site on a typed canvas. The Brain validates the design, compiles it, and 
saves it as an immutable versioned release. One universal renderer turns the release into the 
interface, and one generic API runs the workflows. PostgreSQL keeps every site’s data 
isolated. 
The promise: a new site is a new configuration. It never needs new code. 
The pitch line: “Three units used to mean 36 implementations. We built 13 modules once, 
and configuration does the rest.” 
Our positioning against the other teams: most will show that sites can differ. We show that 
they are also safe (isolated, transactional), publishable live (no restart), and provable (tests 
and panels on screen). 
 
3. Architecture

Postgres or Supabase? Docker Postgres is primary because the demo must work without 
internet and the PS requires its own JWT and bcrypt login. The schema stays 
Supabase-compatible if you want a hosted URL. If you use Supabase, always connect as the 
app_user role, because the default roles can bypass RLS.

reject invalid designs.

The three demo sites are just three configurations of these modules: 
●​ Central Stores: Inventory + Analytics + Excel (sidebar shell, table layouts). 
●​ Service Centre: Request → Approval → PCB Repair, with Returns published live 
(top-bar shell, kanban and queue layouts). 
●​ Distribution: Request → Material Assignment → Consumption (workbench shell, 
split layouts). 
 
5. MVP: the features that must be flawless 
For each: what it is, a concrete example, and why it matters. If any of these is weak, nothing 
else counts. 
MVP 1: One login, many sites, and a Hub admin view 
What: A single login issues a JWT carrying the user’s siteIds and per-site roles. A site 
switcher loads each site with its own theme, pages and data. The Hub shows an admin’s 
combined view across sites.​
 Example: An admin logs in once, sees KPI cards for all three units, and clicks Service 
Centre to enter it. No second login.​


----- PAGE 36 -----
 Why it matters: This removes the “separate logins, no combined view” problem directly, 
and it is demo step 1. 
MVP 2: The single universal renderer 
What: One React renderer reads a site’s JSONB config and produces the shell, the page 
layout, the forms and the tables. There are no site-specific React files.​
 Example: Central Stores renders a sidebar and a stock table. Service Centre renders a top 
bar and a kanban board. Same renderer, different config.​
 Why it matters: It is the heart of the PS and the answer to the hardcoded if/else trap. CI fails 
if a site slug appears anywhere in the source. 
MVP 3: Layout-changing sites (the anti colour-palette answer) 
What: Config selects one of three shells (sidebar, topbar-cards, workbench) and one of seven 
page layouts (table, queue, kanban, split, form, dashboard, report). Theme tokens are the 
smallest difference.​
 Example: Distribution shows a split pane with the request on the left and a stock availability 
meter on the right. Central Stores shows a plain table.​
 Why it matters: The organizer states that swapping CSS variables is not enough. The DOM 
structure must physically change. 
MVP 4: Typed visual builder with presets 
What: A React Flow canvas with a palette of the 13 modules. Connections are typed: an 
output can only connect to an input that accepts it, and each node’s requires must be present. 
Presets (Inventory Starter, Approval Chain, Repair Shop) start a site in one click. An 
inspector panel edits fields, roles and rules through forms.​
 Example: The admin drags Approval onto an empty canvas. The builder refuses with 
“Approval needs a Request node”. After adding Request and connecting it, the edge turns 
green.​
 Why it matters: It is the untyped-canvas trap, and the organizer states that a builder only 
developers can use misses the point. No raw JSON is ever shown. 
MVP 5: Live publish with versions, preview, rollback and deploy history 
What: The builder saves a draft. Validation passes, then Preview shows the draft rendered 
live, then Publish creates an immutable release and flips the site’s active-release pointer. The 
runtime reads the active release on every request. Rollback flips the pointer back. Each 
publish and rollback adds a deploy-history row.​
 Example: The admin adds the Returns module to Service Centre, previews, publishes, and 
the Returns tab appears on the live site within seconds. Rollback makes it disappear, and no 
transaction is changed.​
 Why it matters: It is demo step 3 and the server-restart trap. A restart or frontend rebuild 
fails the requirement. 


----- PAGE 37 -----
MVP 6: Strict site isolation at ORM and database level 
What: Every business table has site_id uuid NOT NULL. In the ORM layer, a 
scoped(siteId) wrapper injects the site filter into every query. In the database layer, 
PostgreSQL row-level security runs under an app_user role that cannot bypass RLS, and 
FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY is on. The site list comes only from the signed JWT, 
never from the request body.​
 Example: A Central Stores user calls the Service Centre requests endpoint and gets 403. 
Even if a developer forgets a WHERE clause, the database returns only the rows for the 
user’s sites.​
 Why it matters: The organizer requires a test for every endpoint proving that data never 
leaks. A common mistake is connecting as the Postgres superuser, where RLS silently does 
nothing. 
MVP 7: Transactional stock (request → approve → assign) 
What: Assignment runs in a true BEGIN … COMMIT block with SELECT … FOR 
UPDATE row locks on the request and the inventory item. Stock lives in an append-only 
ledger (stock_movements) plus a qty_on_hand column guarded by CHECK (qty_on_hand >= 
0). An idempotency key prevents double deduction.​
 Example: 50 users try to assign 1 unit each when only 10 units exist. Exactly 10 succeed, 40 
get “Insufficient stock”, and the ledger sums to the stock.​
 Why it matters: The organizer says approval or assignment “can never leave inventory in 
an inconsistent state”. It is a pass-or-fail criterion. 
MVP 8: Calculated fields with schema validation 
What: Fields can be of type calculated with an expression like qty * price. Expressions are 
parsed with jsep and walked against a whitelist (numbers, field names, arithmetic, 
comparisons, ROUND, MIN, MAX, ABS). No eval. The compiler also builds a Zod schema 
from the field config to validate every request.​
 Example: The request form shows TOTAL = QTY × PRICE live. A formula like 
qty.constructor is rejected with a message.​
 Why it matters: It is an explicit objective, and the sandbox shows security awareness. 
MVP 9: Role-restricted Excel import and export 
What: ExcelJS export and import driven by config. Each report defines exportRoles, 
importRoles and maskColumns per role. Imports validate each row with the compiled 
schema, run in one transaction (all or nothing), and return a row-by-row error report.​
 Example: An admin exports requests with price and total. A staff user exports the same 
report with those columns removed, and a staff import attempt returns 403.​
 Why it matters: It is demo step 4, and “role-based restrictions” is written into the 
objectives. 
MVP 10: Seed data and an offline backup 


----- PAGE 38 -----
What: Three sites, 100+ rows, and one user per role per site. A real pg_dump backup script, 
a pg_restore script tested on a clean database, and an admin “Download backup” button.​
 Example: If the laptop’s database dies before judging, one command restores the demo state.​
 Why it matters: It is a listed deliverable. A browser cache alone does not satisfy a “backup” 
requirement, so we ship both. 
MVP 11 (should-have): Consumption, Returns, PCB Repair and three analytics charts 
What: The remaining modules, implemented through the same module framework, not as 
hand-built screens. Returns has a USABLE/DAMAGED inspection. USABLE adds stock 
back through the ledger and DAMAGED does not. PCB Repair is a six-column kanban. 
Analytics shows requests by status, stock levels and approval turnaround.​
 Example: A damaged return is recorded and stock stays unchanged. A usable one increases 
stock by 1.​
 Why it matters: All 13 modules must exist, and Returns is the live-publish demo module. 
 
6. USPs: what sets us apart from the 500 other teams 
USP 1: Clash-safe editing (field-by-field merge) 
What: Two admins can edit the same site’s draft. Each save carries the version it started 
from. If the draft has changed meanwhile, the server runs a three-way merge on flattened 
JSON paths: changes that touch different fields merge automatically, and only fields changed 
differently by both people need a human choice.​
 Example: Admin A renames a field label while Admin B adds a role. Both changes merge 
silently. If both rename the same label differently, a dialog shows “mine vs theirs”.​
 Importance: It solves “any edit to shared code can break another site” at the config level 
and makes versioned releases safe for teams. Most teams will skip it. 
USP 2: The isolation pen-test panel 
What: A Hub page, shown in demo mode, that runs live attacks. Test A (app layer): a 
Central Stores user hits the Service Centre endpoint and the server returns 403. Test B 
(database layer): a query with no WHERE clause returns only the rows visible to the user, 
shown as 120 dots with only 34 lit. Test C (terminal): psql as app_user with no site set 
returns 0 rows.​
 Example: The judge sees “rows in table: 120, rows visible to you: 34” next to a dot grid.​
 Importance: It makes the core objective visible. Note the honest framing: the 403 comes 
from Express and the filtering comes from Postgres, so we show them as two separate proofs. 
USP 3: Concurrency proof panel 
What: One button fires 50 parallel assignment requests for 10 units and shows the outcome: 
10 successes, 40 rejections, stock never below 0, ledger reconciled.​


----- PAGE 39 -----
 Example: A counter turns green at 10 OK and red at 40 rejected.​
 Importance: It proves ACID behaviour in seconds, with evidence rather than a claim. 
USP 4: Panic Button (resilience) 
What: Zustand persists to IndexedDB (via idb-keyval), keyed per user and cleared on 
logout. A /api/health ping every 5 seconds, plus the browser’s online and offline events as a 
hint, detects an outage. When the backend is unreachable the app shows an “Offline mode: 
viewing cached data” banner, serves cached pages read-only, and disables write buttons. A 
service worker (stretch) lets the app reload offline.​
 Example: During the demo the presenter stops the backend. The banner appears and 
Inventory, PCB Repair and Analytics still render. On restart, the banner disappears.​
 Importance: It converts the mandatory backup deliverable into a product feature. Stopping 
the server is a stronger demo than turning off Wi-Fi, because a localhost backend keeps 
working when Wi-Fi is off. Per-user cache keys keep the isolation story consistent. 
USP 5: AI co-pilot behind a validator 
What: The admin types a business description. The LLM returns a structured config draft 
only. The compiler validates it, and errors are fed back for up to three repair rounds. A 
human reviews the diff before publishing. If the API fails, a cached fallback draft is used.​
 Example: “A returns process where damaged items are inspected before restocking” 
produces a Request → Returns graph with an inspection field, and the builder opens it for 
review.​
 Importance: It makes the builder usable by non-developers without giving the AI any 
ability to publish or bypass validation. 
USP 6: Aging SLA indicators 
What: The server computes age_seconds from status_changed_at for PENDING requests, so 
a wrong laptop clock can’t break it. The UI uses a one-second useNow tick, draws a 
countdown ring, and turns the row red with a text “SLA breached” badge. The limit comes 
from server config (SLA_SECONDS: 86400 in production, 10 for the demo, stated aloud).​
 Example: A new request is created, and 10 seconds later its row turns red without a refresh.​
 Importance: It shows the workflow engine tracks state over time. It sits inside the required 
Approval module, so it is not scope creep. 
USP 7: Cross-site search (Ctrl+K) 
What: /api/hub/search is Admin-only and runs a single query across inventory, requests and 
PCB repairs inside the same tenant transaction, so RLS limits results to the admin’s sites. 
Each result has an origin badge (site and module) and deep-links to 
/s/:slug/:module?highlight=:id, where the row scrolls into view and pulses.​
 Example: Typing “resistor” shows hits from Central Stores and Service Centre. Clicking one 
opens the right site and the right row.​


----- PAGE 40 -----
 Importance: It directly answers “admins get no combined view across units”. It is tier A, so 
build it only after the S-tier items are solid. 
USP 8: Config-pinned transactions 
What: Every request and ledger row stores the config version it ran under. Rollback only 
moves a pointer and never touches business data.​
 Example: After rolling back from v4 to v3, past requests still show they were processed 
under v4.​
 Importance: It makes releases auditable and explains why rollback is safe. 
USP 9: Decision trace and audit log 
What: An append-only audit_log records who did what, on which entity, under which config 
version, with details. Updates and deletes are blocked by trigger.​
 Example: A rejected assignment shows the stock value and rule that caused the rejection.​
 Importance: It makes every failure explainable without reading code, which the PS 
highlights as a need.

Scope note: The PS freezes the scope to the 13 modules. Each extra maps to a required 
objective (pen-test to isolation tests, backup to the offline deliverable, SLA to the Approval 
workflow, search to the cross-unit view, co-pilot to the non-developer builder). Send this 
mapping to the guide today and get written approval for the medium-risk items: search and 
co-pilot.

8. Backend specification 
8.1 Structure 
server/src/ 
  app.ts 
  db/          pool, withTenant, scoped 
  middleware/  auth, requireRole, requireSite, validateBody 
  config/      registry, graphValidator, compiler, expr, release, merge 
  runtime/     resourceRouter, workflow, stock, excel, sla 
  routes/      hub, sites, builder, runtime, search, demo, health 
  ai/          copilot, fallbackDraft.json 
migrations/    001_base, 002_rls, 003_seed 
scripts/       backup.sh, restore.sh 
tests/         rls, isolation matrix, stock concurrency, graph, expression 
8.2 Request pipeline 
JWT verify → requireSite (URL site must be in the token's siteIds, else 403) → requireRole (from 
config) → validateBody (Zod compiled from the release) → withTenant (BEGIN, set_config, query, 
COMMIT) → audit_log 
Rules: 
●​ Site ids come only from the signed token. 
●​ No route calls pool.query directly (an ESLint rule bans it). 


----- PAGE 46 -----
●​ Every query goes through scoped(siteId) (ORM layer), and RLS is the second layer. 
8.3 How live publish works (no restart) 
1.​ The builder saves a draft with an optimistic version number. 
2.​ POST /validate runs the checks in 8.4. 
3.​ POST /publish, in one transaction, inserts an immutable release, sets sites.active_release_id, 
and writes a deploy-history row. 
4.​ The runtime resolves site → active release → config on each request. Compiled schemas are 
cached by release id, which is safe because releases never change. 
5.​ The renderer polls /config/version and re-fetches when it changes. 
6.​ Rollback flips the pointer only. 
8.4 Graph validator rules 
Rule 
Example message 
Every node’s requires must be present 
“Approval needs a Request node” 
Output type must be accepted by the target input 
“Chart cannot feed Request” 
No cycles except declared workflow loops 
“Cycle: inventory → request → 
inventory” 
Every page role must exist 
“Role ‘manager’ is not defined” 
Formulas parse, reference real fields, and have no 
cycles 
“total refers to itself” 
8.5 Expression sandbox 
Parse with jsep, then walk the tree against a whitelist: literals, field names, + - * / %, comparisons, 
&& || !, ternary, and ROUND, MIN, MAX, ABS. Reject member access, constructor, depth above 8, 
and anything else. Tests include qty.constructor and (()=>1)(). 
8.6 Assignment transaction (row locking) 
Inside one transaction: lock the request (FOR UPDATE, must be APPROVED), lock the inventory 
row (FOR UPDATE), reject if qty_on_hand < qty, update stock, insert a ledger row with the config 
version and idempotency key, insert the assignment, set the request to ASSIGNED. A unique 
violation on the idempotency key returns the original result and never deducts twice.​
 Returns: USABLE adds a RETURN movement (+qty). DAMAGED records the return and adds 
nothing. 
8.7 Clash-safe merge algorithm 
Save sends base_version. If it matches the current draft, save and bump. Otherwise load the base 
snapshot, the current draft (theirs) and the incoming config (mine), flatten each to path/value maps, 
and decide per path: only mine changed → mine; only theirs changed → theirs; both changed to the 
same value → that value; both changed differently → conflict. Return {merged, conflicts[]}. 


----- PAGE 47 -----
8.8 Excel engine 
Export requires the role to be in exportRoles and removes maskColumns[role]. Import requires 
importRoles (otherwise 403), validates every row with the compiled schema, runs all-or-nothing in 
one transaction, and returns row-level errors. Each run is stored in excel_jobs.

8.10 Database design 
Platform tables: users (global identity, bcrypt hash), sites (slug, active release), user_sites (role per 
site), module_catalog (the 13 modules with requires, inputs and outputs), site_drafts, draft_snapshots, 
site_releases (immutable JSONB), deploy_history. 
Business tables (all have site_id NOT NULL): inventory_items, stock_movements (append-only), 
requests (with status_changed_at and config_version), approvals, assignments, consumption_logs, 
item_returns, pcb_repairs, excel_jobs, audit_log (append-only). 
Integrity features: 
●​ Composite foreign keys (id, site_id) make cross-site references impossible, so a request can 
never point to another site’s item. 
●​ CHECK (qty_on_hand >= 0) stops negative stock. 
●​ Triggers update status_changed_at on status change and block updates and deletes on the 
ledger and audit log. 


----- PAGE 48 -----
●​ RLS policy on every tenant table: site_id = ANY(app_site_ids()) with WITH CHECK, plus 
FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY. 
●​ Missing session setting fails closed (zero rows) because the policy function uses 
current_setting('app.site_ids', true). 
●​ The app sets the tenant with set_config('app.site_ids', $1, true) inside the transaction. SET 
LOCAL does not accept bind parameters. 
●​ The app_user role is NOSUPERUSER NOBYPASSRLS, and its grants exclude updates and 
deletes on the ledger and audit tables. 
●​ A create_site function (SECURITY DEFINER, hub-admin check) is the one controlled way 
to create a site, and the admin’s token is re-issued afterward. 
Pre-demo check: SELECT current_user, rolbypassrls FROM pg_roles WHERE rolname = 
current_user; must show rolbypassrls = false.

8.12 Backup 
pg_dump -Fc to a timestamped file, pg_restore --clean --if-exists to restore, an admin download 
button, and a restore tested on a clean database before the demo.

10. Business impact (measured, not claimed) 
●​ Time to publish a new module: __ seconds, 0 server restarts. 
●​ Site-specific code lines: 0 (CI check). 
●​ Reuse: 13 modules × 3 sites, 0 forks. 
●​ Isolation: N / N endpoints tested, no leaks. 
●​ Concurrency: 0 oversells in 50 parallel requests. 
Who benefits: warehouses, service centres, manufacturers and distributors with several units and 
workflows that change often. They lower development and maintenance cost, change a form or 
approval chain without a developer, release more safely, and get a combined view across units.​
 For Electrolyte: a platform that can onboard a new client site as configuration instead of a new 
project.​
 Do not claim measured cost savings you haven’t tested. 
 
11. Judge Q&A 
●​ “Isn’t this just different colours?” No. Three shells and seven layouts are chosen by config, 
and the DOM changes. 
●​ “What if the AI is wrong?” It only drafts. The validator can reject it, a human reviews the 
diff, and publishing is manual. 


----- PAGE 50 -----
●​ “How is isolation enforced?” In the ORM wrapper and in Postgres RLS under a role that 
can’t bypass it. We show raw queries failing. 
●​ “Two admins edit at once?” A field-by-field merge resolves non-overlapping edits, and only 
true conflicts need a human. 
●​ “What happens offline?” A read-only IndexedDB cache plus a real database backup and 
tested restore. 
●​ “How is this different from Retool or Odoo?” Retool builds single CRUD apps and Odoo is 
a fixed suite. OpsHub assembles different isolated sites from typed modules and releases them 
live. 
●​ “Why these extras if scope is frozen?” Each maps to a required objective, and we’ve asked 
the guide for approval.

video 
Phases: (1) shared contract, database, auth, skeletons; (2) standalone work on publish, merge, 
co-pilot, SLA and offline; (3) integration, proof panels, polish, five timed rehearsals. 
Shared contract (agree in hour 0–1): only withTenant and scoped touch the database. The JWT 
carries userId, hubRole, siteIds[] and per-site roles. Every tenant table has site_id plus RLS. Requests 
always have status and status_changed_at. The config shape is the only contract between the Brain 
and the Renderer. Every runtime page reads ?highlight=<id>. Logout calls clearCache(). 
Cut order if time runs short: PWA reload offline, then search polish, then pen-test modal polish, 
then the co-pilot (keep the fallback draft). 
Never cut: RLS with a non-bypass role, the typed canvas, live publish with no restart, transactional 
stock, role-restricted Excel, backup and restore, and the fallback video. 
Biggest risks (honest view): 
1.​ The builder plus renderer plus publish pipeline is the hardest integration. Build a thin vertical 
slice early. 
2.​ RLS silently doing nothing because the app connects as the wrong role. Check rolbypassrls 
daily. 


----- PAGE 51 -----
3.​ Over-building extras while a required module is half done. 
4.​ Skipping rehearsal. The demo script is rigid, so timing matters. 
 


----- PAGE 52 -----
My top 5 standout functionalities for OpsHub 
 
1 · Highest priority 
AI-to-Live Workflow Builder 
Let an admin describe a workflow in plain English and turn it into a working application 
configuration. 
Example prompt: 
"Create a material return workflow where an employee submits a request, the manager 
approves it, the storekeeper receives the returned stock, and the manager can track its 
status." 
OpsHub should: 
●​ Generate a draft using your existing Form, Table, Approval, Rule and Report blocks. 
●​ Automatically connect compatible nodes. 
●​ Validate required fields, permissions, rules and workflow connections. 
●​ Show a preview and configuration diff. 
●​ Publish only after the admin confirms. 


----- PAGE 53 -----
Why it stands out: The AI does not merely generate text or code; it creates a usable 
configuration for your universal renderer. 
Build priority: Very high. Extend your existing AI co-pilot rather than building a separate AI 
product. 
 
2 · Best technical differentiator 
Workflow Simulator and Dry Run 
Let admins test a workflow before publishing it. 
For example, simulate a stock request for 20 units when only 12 are available. OpsHub 
should explain that the request cannot be fulfilled and show which rule rejected it. 
Include: 
●​ Step-by-step execution of the workflow. 
●​ Sample inputs and calculated field outputs. 
●​ Approval paths for different conditions. 
●​ Inventory before-and-after previews. 
●​ Clear errors when a rule or configuration is invalid. 
Why it stands out: It demonstrates that your visual builder is an actual workflow engine, not 
just a drag-and-drop UI. 
Important: The simulator must not modify live stock or send real approvals. Use isolated test 
data and a dry-run execution path. 


----- PAGE 54 -----
 
3 · Best reliability feature 
Versioned Publishing with Instant Rollback 
Make configuration changes safe and visible. 
●​ Maintain separate draft and published versions. 
●​ Show exactly which fields, rules and workflow connections changed. 
●​ Identify the author and publication time. 
●​ Restore a previous version without rebuilding the application. 
●​ Keep active requests consistent when the workflow configuration changes. 
Why it stands out: Your PS explicitly emphasizes live publishing, versioned releases, preview 
and rollback. This makes an essential requirement demonstrably reliable, not merely 
claimed. 


----- PAGE 55 -----
 
4 · Best security demonstration 
Live Tenant-Isolation Proof 
Upgrade your existing RLS and pen-test feature into a security evidence dashboard. 
●​ Show the user, permitted sites and attempted resource. 
●​ Display the application-layer 403. 
●​ Separately demonstrate PostgreSQL RLS filtering. 
●​ Show the 34-of-120 accessible-row proof. 
●​ Record test results, timestamps and pass/fail status. 
Why it stands out: Judges can verify your multi-tenant security rather than trusting a slide or 
a screenshot. 
Keep the test isolated and read-only. Never expose unauthorized row contents just to 
demonstrate that access was denied. 


----- PAGE 56 -----
 
5 · Best business-value feature 
Inventory Integrity and Reconciliation 
Make every stock movement traceable. 
●​ Maintain a stock movement ledger for receipts, issues, returns and adjustments. 
●​ Use database transactions and row locking to prevent overselling or negative stock. 
●​ Make repeated requests idempotent so retries do not issue stock twice. 
●​ Show an item's opening balance, movements and closing balance. 
●​ Flag discrepancies between recorded stock and physical counts. 
Why it stands out: It demonstrates production-minded engineering and makes your inventory 
workflows trustworthy. 
Implement this inside the existing Inventory, Assignment and Return modules, not as a new 
module.

Material flaws 
●​ F1: Distribution is invalid under your own validator. You define it as Request → 
Material Assignment → Consumption. But Assignment requires Approval and 
Inventory. This fails when the Distribution preset is loaded and the validator runs: it 
rejects your own demo site. 


----- PAGE 58 -----
●​ F2: Demo step 3 targets the wrong site. The PS script says to enable Returns on 
Distribution. Your §4 and MVP 5 use Service Centre. Judges follow the script. 
●​ F3: No subtype rule. Approval outputs ApprovedRequest, but PCB Repair and 
Returns require RequestRecord. Unless ApprovedRequest counts as a 
RequestRecord, the Request → Approval → Repair chain from the PS cannot be 
drawn. 
●​ F4: The merge engine (USP 1) is overclaimed. Your doc says it solves "any edit to 
shared code can break another site". It doesn't. That problem is solved by 
config-versus-code separation, immutable releases and isolation tests. Flattening 
JSON paths also breaks on arrays: reordering fields on both sides produces false 
conflicts or silent bad merges. The PS does not require concurrent draft editing. 
●​ F5: Scope against 24 hours. The doc has 10 must-haves plus 7 S-tier extras. This 
fails if the gate (login, hub, isolation across two sites) is half-working at hour 12 
because effort went into extras. 
Gaps 
●​ No ORM is named, but the PS says "ORM and database levels". 
●​ It doesn't say how per-site custom fields are stored. 
●​ It doesn't say whether module tables exist before publish. 
●​ Server-side recomputation of calculated fields is unstated. 
●​ Only "per-site roles" is mentioned, not Public, Member, Manager and Admin. 
●​ USP 9 (audit log) has no tier. 
2. Uniqueness rating 
7/10. Configuration-driven multi-tenancy is an established pattern, so the idea alone isn't 
novel. The packaging is: a typed canvas, immutable releases and live proof panels.

OpsHub: Complete Solution Document (10/10 
Edition) 
Genesis 2.0 · PS-03 · Speak a site into existence. Scrub through its history. Run it in a dead zone. 
Try to break it. 
 
1. The problem and what the organizer is judging 
1.1 The problem 
A company runs three units: Central Stores (inventory), Service Centre (requests, approvals, repairs, 
returns) and Distribution (material requests, assignments, consumption). All three need the same basic 
features, but each wants different fields, rules and layouts, so each unit gets its own codebase. Three 
units rebuilding 12 modules means 36 implementations where 12 shared ones would do. 
The consequences are separate logins, no combined view for admins, process owners who can't 
change a form or approval chain without a developer, shared-code edits that break other sites, and 
releases with no preview or rollback. 
1.2 What the organizer expects 
One codebase that runs N completely different sites, in three layers: 
●​ Hub: global JWT + bcrypt login, RBAC, user management, site switcher, theme engine, 
module catalogue. 
●​ Site: each site is {siteId, slug, theme, modules, pages, roles}. 
●​ Module and node: 13 approved modules as typed nodes with defined inputs and outputs. 
1.3 The traps that disqualify 
Trap 
Meaning 
Hardcoded if/else 
Any if (site === 'service-centre') fails the core idea 
Scope creep 
The 13 modules are frozen. Depth beats breadth 
Colour-palette trap 
Switching sites must change layout, modules and workflow, not just 
colours 
Server restart trap 
Publishing a module must need no code change and no restart 
Developer-only builder 
A raw JSON editor misses the point 


----- PAGE 65 -----
Untyped canvas 
Approval must not connect without Request 
1.4 The winning criteria 
●​ Strict site_id isolation at ORM and database level, with a test for every endpoint. 
●​ ACID transactions with row locking for stock. 
●​ One universal renderer driven by JSONB. 
●​ Calculated fields with schema validation. 
●​ A rigid four-step demo. 
●​ Seeded data and an offline backup. 
 
2. Our solution in one page 
OpsHub is a configuration brain with a universal body. 
An admin designs a site (by voice, by typing, or on a typed canvas). The Brain validates the design, 
compiles it, and saves it as an immutable versioned release. One universal renderer turns the release 
into the interface, and one generic API runs the workflows. PostgreSQL keeps every site's data 
isolated. 
The promise: a new site is a new configuration. It never needs new code. 
The pitch line: "Three units used to mean 36 implementations. We built 13 modules once, and 
configuration does the rest." 
What OpsHub is famous for: 
The platform where you speak a business into existence, scrub through its history like a 
video, keep it running in a warehouse dead zone, and invite you to try to break it. 
Product people 
remember

network 
Kisaan Vaani

their language 
Bolo & Build: speak a site into existence in 
Hindi, Marathi or English 
Instagram Reels

Time Slider: drag through versions and watch 
the live site morph 
Positioning: most teams will show that sites can differ. We show that they are also safe (isolated, 
transactional), publishable live (no restart), explorable (rewind any version), and provable (the 
judge can attack it). 


----- PAGE 66 -----
Scope rule: the 13 modules are frozen. Every signature feature below is a new experience of a 
required objective, never a 14th module. 
 
3. Architecture 
Voice / typed prompt ──► Intent layer (LLM returns a config patch only) 
                                   │ 
 ┌──────────────── BRAIN (config engine) ────────────────┐ 
 │ Module registry → Typed graph validator → Compiler    │ 
 │ (Zod + jsep) → Immutable release (JSONB) →            │ 
 │ Active-release pointer · Merge engine ·               │ 
 │ Release diff · Rollback                               │ 
 └───────────┬──────────────────────────┬────────────────┘ 
             ▼                          ▼ 
   Universal renderer            Generic runtime API 
   (also renders any past        /api/:siteId/:resource 
    release read-only)           + idempotent write endpoint 
             └──────────┬───────────────┘ 
                        ▼ 
 PostgreSQL: site_id everywhere · RLS (non-bypass role) · ledger · audit 
                        ▲ 
 Phone PWA: IndexedDB cache + write queue + QR scan 
 Trust Console: attack editor · concurrency · decision trace 
 
Layer 
Components 
Hub 
JWT + bcrypt login, RBAC, users, site switcher, theme engine, module catalogue 
Brain 
Registry, graph validator, jsep sandbox, compiler, release manager, merge engine, 
release diff, intent layer 
Runtime 
Renderer (3 shells × 7 layouts), resource API, workflow engine, stock ledger, Excel 
engine 
Data 
PostgreSQL 16 with RLS, JSONB releases, append-only ledger and audit 
Surrounding 
Offline cache and queue, proof panels, SLA monitor, cross-site search 
Stack: React 18, Vite, TypeScript, Tailwind, React Router, Zustand with IndexedDB persistence, 
Axios, React Flow, Recharts, React Hook Form, Zod, jsep, vite-plugin-pwa / Node, Express, JWT, 
bcrypt, ExcelJS / PostgreSQL 16 in Docker. 
Postgres or Supabase? Docker Postgres is primary, because the demo must work without internet 
and the PS requires its own JWT and bcrypt login. The schema is Supabase-compatible if you want a 
hosted URL, but always connect as the app_user role, because the default roles can bypass RLS.

4. The 13 modules (the building blocks) 
Each module is a typed node with requires, inputs and outputs. The validator uses these to reject 
invalid designs. 
# 
Module 
What it does

The three demo sites are three configurations of these modules: 
●​ Central Stores: Inventory + Analytics + Excel (sidebar shell, table layouts). 
●​ Service Centre: Request → Approval → PCB Repair, with Returns published live (top-bar 
shell, kanban and queue layouts). 


----- PAGE 68 -----
●​ Distribution: Request → Material Assignment → Consumption (workbench shell, split 
layouts). 
 
5. Signature features (S+): why people will remember us 
Signature 1: Bolo & Build (voice-to-site in Hindi, Marathi and English) 
What judges see. The presenter taps a mic and says: "Service Centre mein returns jodo. Kharab 
saman inspection ke baad hi wapas stock mein aaye." On the canvas, nodes appear one by one: 
Request → Returns, with an inspection field and a USABLE/DAMAGED rule. The validator ticks 
each one green. If the AI made a mistake, a chip says "fixed 1 issue automatically". The presenter 
reviews the diff and presses Publish, and the Returns tab appears on the live site. 
How it works. 
1.​ The browser's Speech Recognition API captures speech (hi-IN, mr-IN, en-IN) and returns 
text. Typed input uses the same path. 
2.​ The LLM gets the text plus the module catalogue and must return a config patch as structured 
JSON, limited to known module keys, field types and edges. 
3.​ The compiler validates it (typed ports, requires, formulas, roles). Errors go back to the LLM 
for up to three repair rounds. 
4.​ The canvas animates the validated patch node by node. A human reviews the diff, and only 
the human publishes. 
5.​ If speech or the API fails, a cached fallback draft loads and the typed box still works. 
Example. Saying "approval chain with two approvers above ₹25,000" produces Request → Approval 
with the rule total > 25000 → 2 approvals, evaluated by the same jsep sandbox as every other rule. 
Importance. 
●​ It is the strongest answer to "a builder that only developers can use misses the point". It needs 
no developer, no JSON, and no English. 
●​ It makes live-publish (demo step 3) memorable instead of a drag-and-drop everyone has seen. 
●​ Hindi and Marathi input, shown in Mumbai, is a local-relevance moment generic teams won't 
have. 
Honest risks and fallbacks. 
●​ Browser speech recognition usually needs internet, and a noisy hall hurts accuracy. Use a 
headset mic, pre-test the exact sentences, and keep the typed fallback and a cached draft 
ready. 
●​ Hindi and Marathi accuracy is imperfect, so rehearse and keep the English version ready. 
●​ The AI never publishes automatically. 
Maps to: the non-developer builder objective. Cost: ~6 h. 
 


----- PAGE 69 -----
Signature 2: Time Slider (Release Rewind) 
What judges see. On the Releases page there is a slider with one tick per version. The presenter drags 
it back and the live site morphs: the Returns tab disappears, a field vanishes, the shell shifts. 
Dragging forward brings it back. A plain-English report appears: "v5 → v4: removes 1 module and 2 
fields. 14 open requests keep running under v5 rules." A Rollback button commits the choice. 
How it works. 
●​ Releases are immutable JSONB, so the renderer can draw any release read-only. The slider 
swaps the config the preview frame renders. 
●​ A diff function summarises changes in words (modules, fields, roles, pages). 
●​ The report counts in-flight requests per config version. Every request and ledger row stores its 
config_version. 
●​ Rollback flips the active-release pointer and never touches business data. 
Example. After publishing Returns, the presenter scrubs back ("it's gone"), scrubs forward ("it's back, 
and nothing in the database changed"). 
Importance. 
●​ It turns the dullest required objective (preview, versions, rollback, deploy history) into the 
most visual moment of the demo. 
●​ It answers the organizer's complaint directly: "with no preview or rollback, every release is 
risky". 
●​ The "open requests keep their version" line shows you thought about running businesses, not 
only configuration. 
Risk. Running in-flight requests under their pinned version needs the workflow executor to load a 
release by request.config_version. If time is short, show the count and the pinned version on data rows 
and keep the executor on the active release. 
Maps to: preview, versioned releases, rollback, deploy history. Cost: ~3–4 h. Best wow per hour of 
the five. 
 
Signature 3: Dead-Zone Mode (offline-first field operations) 
What judges see. On a real phone with the PWA installed, the presenter switches on airplane mode. 
An "Offline" banner appears. They scan an item's QR code, the consumption form prefills, and they 
tap Log. The entry sits in the queue as Queued. With airplane mode off it syncs and flips to Synced, 
and the stock ledger on the laptop updates. A second entry that would take stock below zero comes 
back Rejected: insufficient stock, with the reason. 
How it works. 
●​ A write queue in IndexedDB. Each action gets a client-generated idempotency key. 


----- PAGE 70 -----
●​ On reconnect (the health ping plus online events, since Background Sync is not available on 
Safari) the queue replays to the server. A repeated key returns the original result, so nothing is 
ever deducted twice. 
●​ Only two actions work offline: create request and log consumption. Approve and assign 
stay online-only. 
●​ QR scanning uses a camera library, and the QR encodes the item id. 
●​ Reads come from the per-user IndexedDB cache, wiped on logout. 
The principle, said aloud: "Offline can ask. Only the server can decide." Offline clients never 
change stock directly, so integrity holds. 
Importance. 
●​ It is the ResQLink-style moment: a system working where connectivity fails, shown on a real 
phone. 
●​ It turns the mandatory "offline backup so the demo works if setup fails" deliverable into a 
product strength. 
●​ It is practical: distribution teams work in the field and stores work in basements. 
Risks. 
●​ Camera access and PWA install need HTTPS (localhost is exempt), so a phone on your LAN 
needs a tunnel or local certificate. Test this on day one. 
●​ It is the most expensive feature, so it is the first to cut. The read-only cache and the real 
pg_dump backup stay regardless. 
Maps to: the offline backup deliverable, plus Consumption Tracking and Return Management. Cost: 
~7 h. 
 
Signature 4: Trust Console ("Try to break it") 
What judges see. A Hub page where the judge is invited to attack. Three panels: 
1.​ Isolation. The presenter tampers with the site id or token. A Central Stores user hitting 
Service Centre gets 403. Below it, a dot grid shows 120 rows with only the user's 34 lit, from 
a query with no WHERE clause in code. That is the database filtering, not the app. 
2.​ Concurrency. One button fires 50 parallel assignments for 10 units. The counters settle at 10 
succeeded, 40 rejected, stock 0, ledger balanced. 
3.​ Why was I blocked? Every rejection has a "Why?" link that opens its decision trace: the rule, 
the inputs, the config version. 
How it works. Demo-mode-only endpoints, app_user with RLS, FOR UPDATE row locks, an 
idempotency key, and the append-only audit log. The honest framing matters: the 403 comes from 
Express and the filtering comes from Postgres, so show them as two separate proofs. 
Importance. 


----- PAGE 71 -----
●​ Isolation and transactional integrity are the PS's core winning criteria, and most teams will 
only claim them. You make them tangible and let the judge take part. 
●​ Inviting an attack is a confidence signal almost no team will send. 
Risk. Check rolbypassrls = false before every demo, or the proof is fake. 
Maps to: the per-endpoint isolation tests and the transactional-integrity objective. Cost: ~5 h. 
 
Signature 5: One-Save Provisioning 
What judges see. The presenter creates a new site from a preset and saves once. A live log ticks 
through real steps: site registered, draft seeded, config validated, release v1 published, roles assigned, 
endpoints live, isolation verified. A timer shows the total (for example 6.4 s). Then GET 
/api/<new-slug>/requests returns 200 for the site admin and 403 for everyone else. 
How it works. A single transaction (the create_site function plus a v1 publish) does the database 
work. The "endpoints live" step is honest: the generic router resolves routes from config, so nothing is 
registered or restarted. The last step runs a real isolation probe against the new site. 
Importance. 
●​ The PS says "one builder puts together completely different sites, each with its own isolated 
backend, in a single save". This is that sentence made visible. 
●​ It creates the "no restart, no code" feeling with a number judges can see. 
Maps to: the single-save objective. Cost: ~2–3 h. Best used as a 20-second Q&A clip.

6. Foundations (MVP): the baseline that must be flawless 
Nobody remembers these, but missing any one loses you points or the whole event. Each has a 
concrete example and an importance. 
F1: One login, many sites, and a Hub admin view 
What: A single login issues a JWT carrying siteIds and per-site roles. A site switcher loads each site 
with its own theme, pages and data. The Hub shows an admin's combined view.​
 Example: An admin logs in once, sees KPIs for all three units, and clicks Service Centre. No second 
login.​
 Importance: It removes the "separate logins, no combined view" problem and is demo step 1. 
F2: One universal renderer 
What: One React renderer reads a site's JSONB config and produces the shell, layout, forms and 
tables. There are no site-specific React files.​
 Example: Central Stores draws a sidebar and stock table. Service Centre draws a top bar and a 
kanban. Same renderer, different config.​
 Importance: It is the heart of the PS and the answer to the hardcoded-if/else trap. CI fails if a site 
slug appears in source. 
F3: Layout-changing sites 
What: Config selects one of three shells (sidebar, topbar-cards, workbench) and one of seven layouts 
(table, queue, kanban, split, form, dashboard, report). Theme tokens are the smallest difference.​
 Example: Distribution is a split pane with a stock availability meter. Central Stores is a plain table.​
 Importance: The organizer says swapping CSS variables is not enough. The DOM must change. 
F4: Typed visual builder with presets 
What: A React Flow canvas with the 13 modules. An output connects only to an input that accepts it, 
and each node's requires must be present. Presets (Inventory Starter, Approval Chain, Repair Shop) 
start a site in one click. An inspector edits fields, roles and rules through forms, never raw JSON.​
 Example: Dragging Approval onto an empty canvas is refused: "Approval needs a Request node". 
After adding and connecting Request, the edge turns green.​
 Importance: It avoids the untyped-canvas trap and the developer-only-builder trap. 
F5: Live publish with versions, preview, rollback and history 
What: The builder saves a draft. Validation passes, Preview renders the draft live, and Publish creates 
an immutable release and flips the active-release pointer. The runtime reads the active release on 
every request. Rollback flips the pointer back, and each action adds a deploy-history row.​
 Example: The admin adds Returns, publishes, and the tab appears within seconds. No restart.​
 Importance: It is demo step 3 and the server-restart trap. 
F6: Strict isolation at ORM and database level 


----- PAGE 73 -----
What: Every business table has site_id uuid NOT NULL. A scoped(siteId) wrapper injects the filter 
(ORM layer). PostgreSQL row-level security under a non-bypass app_user role, with FORCE ROW 
LEVEL SECURITY, enforces it again (database layer). Site ids come only from the signed JWT.​
 Example: A Central Stores user calling the Service Centre endpoint gets 403. A forgotten WHERE 
clause still returns only the user's rows.​
 Importance: The organizer requires a test for every endpoint. A common mistake is connecting as 
the superuser, where RLS silently does nothing. 
F7: Transactional stock (request → approve → assign) 
What: Assignment runs in a real BEGIN … COMMIT with SELECT … FOR UPDATE on the 
request and the inventory item. An append-only ledger plus CHECK (qty_on_hand >= 0) and an 
idempotency key prevent inconsistency and double deduction.​
 Example: 50 users try to assign 1 unit each when only 10 exist. Exactly 10 succeed.​
 Importance: "Can never leave inventory in an inconsistent state" is pass-or-fail. 
F8: Calculated fields with schema validation 
What: calculated fields hold expressions like qty * price, parsed by jsep and checked against a 
whitelist (numbers, field names, arithmetic, comparisons, ROUND, MIN, MAX, ABS). No eval. The 
compiler builds a Zod schema from the field config to validate every request.​
 Example: The form shows TOTAL = QTY × PRICE live. qty.constructor is rejected.​
 Importance: An explicit objective that also shows security awareness. 
F9: Role-restricted Excel 
What: ExcelJS export and import driven by config. Each report defines exportRoles, importRoles and 
maskColumns per role. Imports validate every row, run all-or-nothing in one transaction, and return a 
row-by-row error report.​
 Example: An admin exports with price and total. Staff exports the same report with those columns 
removed, and a staff import returns 403.​
 Importance: It is demo step 4 and a written objective. 
F10: Seed data and a real offline backup 
What: Three sites, 100+ rows, a user per role per site. A pg_dump script, a pg_restore script tested on 
a clean database, and an admin download button.​
 Example: If the laptop's database dies before judging, one command restores the demo.​
 Importance: It is a listed deliverable. A browser cache alone does not satisfy "backup", so we ship 
both. 
F11: The remaining modules through the framework 
What: Consumption, Returns (USABLE adds stock through the ledger, DAMAGED does not), PCB 
Repair (six-column kanban) and three analytics charts, all built through the same module framework, 
not as hand-built screens.​
 Example: A damaged return is recorded and stock stays unchanged. A usable one adds 1.​
 Importance: All 13 modules must exist, and Returns is the live-publish module. 


----- PAGE 74 -----
